Median eigenvalues of subcubic graphs
Hricha Acharya, Benjamin Jeter, Zilin Jiang

Abstract
We show that the median eigenvalues of every connected graph of maximum degree at most three, except for the Heawood graph, are at most 1 1 in absolute value, resolving open problems posed by Fowler and Pisanski, and by Mohar.



\setlistdepth

7

1
Introduction

A graph is
subcubic
if its maximum degree is at most
3
. In mathematical chemistry, connected subcubic graphs are often referred to as
chemical graphs
because they naturally model the skeletons of organic molecules, where atoms such as carbon typically form bonds with up to three neighboring atoms.

The study of connected subcubic graphs has significant applications in theoretical chemistry, particularly in the analysis of molecular orbital models. For instance, in the Hückel molecular orbital model, the eigenvalues of a molecule’s skeleton graph correspond to its
\pi
-electron energy levels. Among these, the
median eigenvalues
— associated with the highest occupied molecular orbital (HOMO) and the lowest unoccupied molecular orbital (LUMO) — are of particular interest (see
[
11
,
17
]
and the references therein).

To formalize, let
G
be an
n
-vertex graph, and let
\lambda_{1}(G)\geq\lambda_{2}(G)\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{n}(G)
denote the eigenvalues of its adjacency matrix. The
median eigenvalues
are defined as
\lambda_{h}
and
\lambda_{l}
, where
h=\lfloor(n+1)/2\rfloor
and
l=\lceil(n+1)/2\rceil
.

Computational studies of Fowler and Pisanski
[
4
,
5
]
suggest that the median eigenvalues of most connected subcubic graphs lie in the interval
[-1,1]
. However, there is a single notable exception: the Heawood graph, whose median eigenvalues are
\pm\sqrt{2}
(see
Figure

1
). The Heawood graph is the incidence graph of the Fano plane, that is, the projective plane of order
2
.

Figure 1
:
The Heawood graph.

Fowler and Pisanski
[
4
]
conjectured that the median eigenvalues of all but finitely many connected subcubic graphs lie in
[-1,1]
, and they confirmed their conjecture for all connected subcubic trees. The interval
[-1,1]
would be optimal because of the construction of an infinite family of bipartite connected subcubic graphs whose median eigenvalues are
\pm 1
by Guo and Mohar
[
6
]
.

Subsequently, Mohar
[
13
]
conjectured that the median eigenvalues of all
planar
subcubic graphs lie in
[-1,1]
, and he confirmed in
[
12
]
his conjecture for all
bipartite planar
subcubic graphs. We point out that the Heawood graph is bipartite but not planar.

Later, Mohar
[
14
]
greatly extended his result to all
bipartite
connected subcubic graphs, except for the Heawood graph. In addition, he refined his proof to show that a positive fraction of the eigenvalues around the median eigenvalues belong to
[-1,1]
(see
[
14
, Theorem 1.4]
). Recently, Wang and Zhang
[
16
]
supplemented Mohar’s main result in
[
14
]
by treating connected subcubic graphs that contain
K_{2,3}
as a subgraph, as well as those that contain no
K_{4}
as a minor. The latter generalizes an earlier result of Benediktovich
[
2
]
for outerplanar connected subcubic graphs. Concurrent with our work, Chen, Wang and Zhang
[
3
]
proved that the median eigenvalues lie in
[-1,1]
for connected subcubic graphs in which every subgraph has average degree less than
44/17
, and also for planar connected subcubic graphs of girth at least
8
.

We completely settle the aforementioned conjectures in a strong form.

Theorem 1.1
.

The median eigenvalues of every connected subcubic graph, except for the Heawood graph, lie in the interval
[-1,1]
.

Just like Mohar’s result
[
14
, Theorem 1.4]
, our proof can also be extended to a positive fraction of the eigenvalues around the medians for large connected subcubic graphs.

Theorem 1.2
.

There exists a constant
\eta>0
such that for every connected subcubic graph
G
on
n
vertices, the
i
-th largest eigenvalue
\lambda_{i}(G)
of
G
lies in
[-1,1]
for every integer
i\in[\tfrac{1}{2}(1-\eta)(n+1),\tfrac{1}{2}(1+\eta)(n+1)]
.

Remark
.

When
G
is the Heawood graph, or more generally, when
G
is a small graph on an even number of vertices,
Theorem

1.2
holds for the trivial reason that
[\tfrac{1}{2}(1-\eta)(n+1),\tfrac{1}{2}(1+\eta)(n+1)]
contains no integers.

2
Proof ideas

The use of the Cauchy interlacing theorem in the context of median eigenvalues of connected subcubic graphs first appeared in
[
12
]
. We use the following immediate consequence of the Cauchy interlacing theorem to control graph eigenvalues.

Definition 2.1

(Tails and their estimates)
.

Given a graph
G
, its
positive tail
, denoted by
t^{+}_{G}
, is the number of eigenvalues greater than
1
, and its
negative tail
, denoted by
t^{-}_{G}
, is the number of eigenvalues less than
-1
. Furthermore, given a vertex subset
A
of
G
, the
positive tail estimate
of
G
with respect to
A
is defined by

t^{+}_{G}(A):=\lvert A\rvert+t^{+}_{G-A},

and the
negative tail estimate
of
G
with respect to
A
is defined by

t^{-}_{G}(A):=\lvert A\rvert+t^{-}_{G-A}.

Lemma 2.2
.

For every vertex subset
A
of a graph
G
, the positive tail
t^{+}_{G}
of
G
is at most the positive tail estimate
t^{+}_{G}(A)
, and the negative tail
t^{-}_{G}
of
G
is at most the negative tail estimate
t^{-}_{G}(A)
.

Proof.

Set
k=t_{G-A}^{+}
. Thus
\lambda_{k+1}(G-A)\leq 1
. By the Cauchy interlacing theorem,
\lambda_{\lvert A\rvert+k+1}(G)\leq 1
, and so
t_{G}^{+}\leq\lvert A\rvert+k=t_{G}^{+}(A)
. Similarly, one can also show that
t^{-}_{G}\leq t^{-}_{G}(A)
.
∎

The maximum cut naturally provides a good starting point for tail estimates.

Proposition 2.3
.

For every maximum cut
(A,B)
of a subcubic graph
G
, the edges of
G[A]\cup G[B]
form a matching in
G
, and, in particular,
t_{G}^{\pm}(A)=\lvert A\rvert
and
t_{G}^{\pm}(B)=\lvert B\rvert
.

Proof.

Suppose that some vertex
v\in A
has at least two neighbors in
A
. Since
G
is subcubic,
v
has at most one neighbor in
B
. Moving
v
from
A
to
B
therefore increases the size of the cut-set, contradicting the maximality of
(A,B)
. The same argument applies to vertices in
B
, and so every vertex has degree at most
1
in
G[A]\cup G[B]
. Hence the edges of
G[A]\cup G[B]
form a matching in
G
.

In particular,
G[A]
and
G[B]
are disjoint unions of isolated vertices and edges, so all their eigenvalues lie in
[-1,1]
. Therefore
t^{\pm}_{G-A}=t^{\pm}_{G[B]}=0
and
t^{\pm}_{G-B}=t^{\pm}_{G[A]}=0
, which gives
t_{G}^{\pm}(A)=\lvert A\rvert
and
t_{G}^{\pm}(B)=\lvert B\rvert
.
∎

If
\lvert A\rvert\neq\lvert B\rvert
for some maximum cut
(A,B)
of
G
, then
Lemmas

2.2
and
2.3
imply that
t_{G}^{\pm}\leq\min(\lvert A\rvert,\lvert B\rvert)<\lvert G\rvert/2
, and so the median eigenvalues of
G
lie in
[-1,1]
. It remains to handle graphs whose maximum cuts are all balanced, that is,
\lvert A\rvert=\lvert B\rvert
for every maximum cut
(A,B)
of
G
. The remaining case requires more work.

The strategy is to adjust the maximum cut to improve the tail estimates of
G
. This follows the philosophy of Mohar’s imbalance argument
[
14
]
. We package the adjustments that decrease tail estimates in the following definitions.

Definition 2.4

(Tail reducers)
.

Given a cut
(A,B)
of a graph
G
, a vertex subset
D
of
G
is a
positive tail reducer
(respectively,
negative tail reducer
) of
G
with respect to
(A,B)
if either
D\subseteq A
or
D\subseteq B
, and
t_{Q}^{+}<\lvert D\rvert
(respectively,
t_{Q}^{-}<\lvert D\rvert
), where
Q
is the union of the connected components of
G[D^{\prime}]
that contain vertices in
D
, and

D^{\prime}=\begin{cases}D\cup B&\text{if }D\subseteq A;\\
D\cup A&\text{if }D\subseteq B.\\
\end{cases}

We say that
D
is an
absolute tail reducer
if it is both a positive tail reducer and a negative tail reducer.

As the name suggests, a tail reducer decreases the corresponding tail estimate.

Lemma 2.5

(Lemma 2.3 of Mohar
[
14
]
)
.

For every cut
(A,B)
of a graph
G
, and every vertex subset
D
of
G
, if
D
is a positive tail reducer with respect to
(A,B)
, then

t^{+}_{G}(A\setminus D)<t^{+}_{G}(A)\text{ or }t^{+}_{G}(B\setminus D)<t^{+}_{G}(B).

Likewise, if
D
is a negative tail reducer with respect to
(A,B)
, then

t^{-}_{G}(A\setminus D)<t^{-}_{G}(A)\text{ or }t^{-}_{G}(B\setminus D)<t^{-}_{G}(B).

Although
[
14
, Lemma 2.3]
only concerns positive tail reducers, the proof for negative tail reducers is almost verbatim. Since the terminology in
[
14
]
is different from ours, we include the proof here for the reader’s convenience.

Proof.

We only prove the case where
D\subseteq A
is a positive tail reducer with respect to
(A,B)
. Let
Q
be the union of the connected components of
G[D\cup B]
that contain vertices in
D
. By
Definition

2.4
, we have
t^{+}_{Q}<\lvert D\rvert
, and therefore

t_{G}^{+}(A\setminus D)=\lvert A\setminus D\rvert+t^{+}_{G-(A\setminus D)}=\lvert A\rvert-\lvert D\rvert+t^{+}_{G[D\cup B]}\leq\lvert A\rvert-\lvert D\rvert+t^{+}_{Q}+t^{+}_{G[B]}<\lvert A\rvert+t^{+}_{G[B]}=t^{+}_{G}(A).

The other cases can be proved similarly.
∎

To decrease the positive tail estimate of a
bipartite
connected subcubic graph, Mohar proved the following result. We denote by
G(x,r)
the subgraph of
G
on the vertices within distance
r
from
x
, where
x
could be a vertex, an edge, or a vertex subset.

Lemma 2.6

(Lemma 3.1 of Mohar
[
14
]
)
.

For every bipartite connected subcubic graph
G
other than the Heawood graph, and every vertex
v
of
G
, if
(A,B)
is the bipartition of
G
, then there exists a vertex subset
C
of
G(v,17)
such that
C
is an absolute tail reducer of
G
with respect to
(A,B)
. ∎

Remark
.

In
Lemma

2.6
, since
G
is bipartite, the spectrum of an induced subgraph of
G
is symmetric with respect to
0
, and so a vertex subset
C
is a positive tail reducer if and only if
C
is a negative tail reducer.

The non-bipartite setting introduces two difficulties that do not occur in
[
14
]
. First, a maximum cut need not be unique or canonical, so the proof must exploit only local consequences of maximality. Second, without bipartite spectral symmetry, a set that reduces the positive tail estimate need not reduce the negative tail estimate. This is why our main local work comes in two parallel forms, one for
t_{G}^{+}
and one for
t_{G}^{-}
.

Lemma 2.7
.

For every maximum cut
(A,B)
of a subcubic graph
G
, and for every edge
\alpha
of
G[A]\cup G[B]
, there exists a vertex subset of
G(\alpha,16)
that is a positive tail reducer of
G
with respect to
(A,B)
.

Lemma 2.8
.

For every maximum cut
(A,B)
of a subcubic graph
G
, and for every edge
\alpha
of
G[A]\cup G[B]
, there exists a vertex subset of
G(\alpha,14)
that is a negative tail reducer of
G
with respect to
(A,B)
.

We are ready to prove our first main theorem.

Proof of
Theorem

1.1
assuming
Lemmas

2.7
and
2.8
.

Let
G
be an
n
-vertex connected subcubic graph that is not the Heawood graph, and let
(A,B)
be a maximum cut of
G
; if
G
is bipartite, choose
(A,B)
to be a bipartition. By
Proposition

2.3
, we have

t^{\pm}_{G}(A)\leq\lvert A\rvert\text{ and }t^{\pm}_{G}(B)\leq\lvert B\rvert.

If
\lvert A\rvert\neq\lvert B\rvert
,
Lemma

2.2
implies that

t^{\pm}_{G}\leq\min(t^{\pm}_{G}(A),t^{\pm}_{G}(B))\leq\min(\lvert A\rvert,\lvert B\rvert)<\lvert G\rvert/2.

From now on, assume that
\lvert A\rvert=\lvert B\rvert
. If
G
is bipartite, then
Lemma

2.6
provides an absolute tail reducer with respect to its bipartition. If
G
is not bipartite, then
G[A]\cup G[B]
contains an edge, and
Lemmas

2.7
and
2.8
provide a positive tail reducer and a negative tail reducer with respect to
(A,B)
. In either case,
Lemma

2.5
gives a set whose removal from
A
or from
B
strictly decreases the corresponding tail estimate. Applying
Lemma

2.2
, we obtain

t^{+}_{G}<\lvert G\rvert/2\quad\text{and}\quad t^{-}_{G}<\lvert G\rvert/2,

and hence the median eigenvalues of
G
lie in
[-1,1]
.
∎

It remains to prove the two tail-reducer lemmas by analyzing, case by case, the neighborhoods of an edge of
G[A]\cup G[B]
. We begin with a maximum cut
(A,B)
and repeatedly use local consequences of its maximality. Throughout the remainder of the paper,
\oplus
denotes symmetric difference.

Definition 2.9

(Cut enhancer)
.

Given a cut
(A,B)
of a graph
G
, a vertex subset
C
is a
cut enhancer
if the cut-set of
(A\oplus C,B\oplus C)
is larger than that of
(A,B)
.

A maximum cut therefore admits no cut enhancer. This observation yields many of the local restrictions used below.

To streamline the case analysis, we often modify the initial maximum cut
(A,B)
so that a given case reduces to one already considered. We formalize this operation as follows.

Definition 2.10

(Cut preserver and flipping)
.

Given a cut
(A,B)
of a graph
G
, a vertex subset
C
of
G
is a
cut preserver
if the cut-set of
(A\oplus C,B\oplus C)
has the same size as that of
(A,B)
. In this case, passing from
(A,B)
to
(A\oplus C,B\oplus C)
is called
flipping
the cut preserver
C
.

We shall only make use of the following cut preserver.

Proposition 2.11
.

For every cut
(A,B)
of a graph
G
, and every edge
ab
of
G
with
a\in A
and
b\in B
, if both
a
and
b
have degree
3
,
a
has exactly one neighbor in
A
, and
b
has exactly one neighbor in
B
, then
\left\{{a,b}\right\}
is a cut preserver. ∎

In our application,
(A,B)
is a maximum cut of a subcubic graph
G
, and the cut preserver
C
satisfies
\lvert A\cap C\rvert=\lvert B\cap C\rvert
(see
Proposition

2.11
). Under these conditions, the next tool produces a tail reducer with respect to
(A,B)
from one with respect to
(A\oplus C,B\oplus C)
.

Lemma 2.12
.

For every maximum cut
(A,B)
of a subcubic graph
G
, and every pair of vertex subsets
C
and
D
of
G
, if
C
is a cut preserver with
\lvert A\cap C\rvert=\lvert B\cap C\rvert
, and
D
is a positive (respectively, negative) tail reducer with respect to
(A\oplus C,B\oplus C)
, then
(C\cup D)\cap A
or
(C\cup D)\cap B
is a positive (respectively, negative) tail reducer with respect to
(A,B)
.

B\oplus C
A\oplus C
D
C

Figure 2
:
A Venn diagram for
Lemma

2.12
, where white represents
A
and gray represents
B
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
D\subseteq A\oplus C
, and
D
is a positive tail reducer with respect to
(A\oplus C,B\oplus C)
. Let
Q
be the union of the connected components of
G[D\cup(B\oplus C)]
that contain vertices in
D
. Since
(A\oplus C,B\oplus C)
is also a maximum cut,
Proposition

2.3
implies that the maximum degree of
G[B\oplus C]
is at most
1
. Thus

t^{+}_{G[D\cup(B\oplus C)]}=t^{+}_{Q}<\lvert D\rvert.

The Cauchy interlacing theorem implies that

t^{+}_{G[D\cup(B\oplus C)\cup(B\cap C)]}\leq t^{+}_{G[D\cup(B\oplus C)]}+\lvert(B\cap C)\setminus D\rvert<\lvert D\rvert+\lvert(B\cap C)\setminus D\rvert.

Set
D^{\prime}=(C\cup D)\cap A
. From
Figure

2
and the assumption
\lvert A\cap C\rvert=\lvert B\cap C\rvert
, we deduce that

\displaystyle D\cup(B\oplus C)\cup(C\setminus(A\cup D))=D^{\prime}\cup B,

\displaystyle\lvert D\rvert+\lvert(B\cap C)\setminus D\rvert=\lvert D\setminus C\rvert+\lvert B\cap C\rvert=\lvert D\setminus C\rvert+\lvert A\cap C\rvert=\lvert D^{\prime}\rvert.

Let
Q^{\prime}
be the union of the connected components of
G[D^{\prime}\cup B]
that contain vertices in
D^{\prime}
.
Proposition

2.3
implies that the maximum degree of
G[B]
is at most
1
, and so
t^{+}_{Q^{\prime}}=t^{+}_{G[D^{\prime}\cup B]}<\lvert D^{\prime}\rvert
. Thus
D^{\prime}
is a positive tail reducer with respect to
(A,B)
.
∎

Remark
.

The proof above actually shows that, in
Lemma

2.12
, if in addition
D\subseteq A\oplus C
, then
(C\cup D)\cap A
is a positive (respectively, negative) tail reducer with respect to
(A,B)
.

To facilitate case analysis, we introduce the following organizational tool that tracks the interaction between the edges outside the cut-set.

Definition 2.13

(Underlying multigraph)
.

Given a cut
(A,B)
of a graph
G
, the
underlying multigraph

M
of
G
with respect to
(A,B)
is the multigraph on the edges of
G[A]\cup G[B]
, where
k
edges are placed between
\alpha
and
\beta
in
M
if the bipartite graph
G[\alpha,\beta]
contains
k
edges.

Recall from
Proposition

2.3
that, for every maximum cut
(A,B)
of a subcubic graph
G
, the edges of
G[A]\cup G[B]
form a matching. Hence every edge of the underlying multigraph joins an edge of
G[A]
to an edge of
G[B]
, so the underlying multigraph is bipartite.

Proposition 2.14
.

For every maximum cut
(A,B)
of a subcubic graph
G
, the underlying multigraph of
G
with respect to
(A,B)
is bipartite. ∎

The proofs of
Lemmas

2.7
and
2.8
follow the same outline. Because
M
is bipartite, any relevant neighborhood that is simple, has maximum degree at most
2
, and is not an isolated vertex must be a path or an even cycle. The path lemmas
Lemmas

4.5
and
5.7
handle long paths and long even cycles uniformly; separate lemmas in
Sections

4
and
5
cover the remaining short cases. These arguments either directly exhibit one of the finitely many positive or negative tail reducers displayed in
Figures

6
and
8
, or first flip cut preservers and then transfer the resulting tail reducer back to the original maximum cut using
Lemma

2.12
.

The arguments in
Sections

4
and
5
reduce the proof to a finite analysis of local configurations, with each case subject to precise distinctness, inducedness, and degree conditions. This makes this part of the argument particularly amenable to formal verification. As an independent check of the case analysis and its use in
Lemmas

2.7
and
2.8
, we developed a Lean 4 formalization using Codex 0.146 with OpenAI’s GPT-5.6 Sol model at high reasoning effort. The formalization verifies the relevant local statements and the completeness of the case analysis; it does not formalize the entire paper.

Developing the formalization also served as a useful audit of the mathematical argument: it identified several minor omissions in an earlier version of the manuscript. All of these issues have been corrected in the present version. We reviewed the formal statements and confirmed that the ancillary Lean project builds successfully.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In
Section

3
, we introduce common notation, the concrete cut enhancers used later, basic local degree constraints, and the elementary facts about the underlying multigraph. In
Sections

4
and
5
, we prove the positive and negative tail-reducer lemmas, respectively. In
Section

6
, we combine local tail reducers to prove the positive-fraction result,
Theorem

1.2
. In
Appendix

A
, we verify the tail reducers displayed in
Figures

6
and
8
. In
Appendix

B
, we state precisely what is formalized and describe the simplifications used in translating the mathematical argument into Lean.

3
Other common tools

For readability, we use lowercase Latin letters for vertices and lowercase Greek letters for edges throughout the remainder of the paper. We begin with the following convenient terminology.

Definition 3.1

(Red, reddish, blue and bluish)
.

Given a cut
(A,B)
of a graph
G
, we say that the edges of
G[A]
are
red
, and the edges of
G[B]
are
blue
, and moreover, a vertex in
A
is
red
if it is incident with a red edge, and
reddish
otherwise, and a vertex in
B
is
blue
if it is incident with a blue edge, and
bluish
otherwise.

Pictorially, we draw

for a red edge whose endpoints are red, a pastel red circle

for a reddish vertex, a red-outlined circle

for a vertex in
A
,

for a blue edge whose endpoints are blue, a pastel blue square

for a bluish vertex, and a blue-outlined square

for a vertex in
B
.

We emphasize that the above terminology is
context dependent
— as we flip a cut preserver
C
, some vertices and edges might get recolored according to the new cut
(A\oplus C,B\oplus C)
.

Proposition 3.2
.

For every cut
(A,B)
of a subcubic graph
G
, if any of the graphs in
Figure

3
is an induced subgraph
F
of
G
, then the vertices of
F
form a cut enhancer.

\uparrow

(a)

\downarrow
\uparrow
\uparrow

(b)

\uparrow

(c)

\uparrow
\downarrow
\uparrow

(d)

\downarrow
\uparrow
\downarrow
\uparrow
\uparrow

(e)

\downarrow
\downarrow
\uparrow
\uparrow
\uparrow

(f)

\uparrow

(g)

Figure 3
:
Cut enhancers.

Proof.

For each vertex
v
, let
\delta(v)
be the number of edges incident to
v
in the cut-set of
(A\oplus C,B\oplus C)
minus the number of those edges in the cut-set of
(A,B)
. We label each vertex
v
above by
\uparrow
if
\delta(v)\geq 1
, nothing if
\delta(v)\geq 0
, and
\downarrow
if
\delta(v)\geq-1
. Notice that there are more
\uparrow
s than
\downarrow
s.
∎

We shall repeatedly use the following immediate consequence of
Figure

3(a)
and
Proposition

2.3
to deduce colors of vertices.

Lemma 3.3
.

For every maximum cut
(A,B)
of a subcubic graph
G
, if
a
is a red vertex,
bc
is a blue edge, and both
b
and
d
are the neighbors of
a
in
B
, then
d
is bluish.

For certain cut enhancers used only in this section, we require degree constraints on vertices in the ambient graph
G
. To indicate such a constraint, we give the red circle lower corners: one lower-left corner indicates degree
1
, while two lower corners indicate degree
2
.

Proposition 3.4
.

For every cut
(A,B)
of a subcubic graph
G
, if any of the graphs in
Figure

4
is an induced subgraph
F
of
G
satisfying the indicated degree constraint, then the vertices of
F
form a cut enhancer.

\uparrow

(a)

\uparrow

(b)

Figure 4
:
Cut enhancers with degree constraints — the red vertices are of degree
1
and
2
, respectively.

Proof.

For each vertex
v
of
F
, let
\delta(v)
be the change in the number of cut-set edges incident to
v
when
V(F)
is flipped. The symbol
\uparrow
means that
\delta(v)\geq 1
, while no arrow means that
\delta(v)\geq 0
. Thus, flipping
V(F)
increases the size of the cut-set by at least
1
.
∎

Only the following degree-constrained absolute tail reducer is used in the paper, and only in this section.

Lemma 3.5
.

For every cut
(A,B)
of a subcubic graph
G
, if the graph in
Figure

5
is an induced subgraph of
G
satisfying the indicated degree constraint, then
\left\{{a}\right\}
is an absolute tail reducer.

a
b

Figure 5
:
An absolute tail reducer with a degree constraint — the red vertex
a
is of degree
2
.

Proof.

The connected component of
G[\left\{{a}\right\}\cup B]
containing
a
is the edge
ab
, and
t^{\pm}_{G[\left\{{a,b}\right\}]}=0<1
. Thus
\left\{{a}\right\}
is an absolute tail reducer.
∎

The following two lemmas impose degree constraints on subcubic graphs that lack absolute tail reducers.

Lemma 3.6
.

For every maximum cut
(A,B)
of a subcubic graph
G
, if
ab
is a red edge, then
a
is of degree
3
in
G
, or
\left\{{a}\right\}
is an absolute tail reducer.

Proof.

Suppose
a
is of degree at most
2
.
Figure

4(a)
implies that
a
is of degree
2
, because otherwise
\left\{{a}\right\}
is a cut enhancer. Let
c
be the second neighbor of
a
.
Proposition

2.3
implies that
c\in B
, and
Figure

4(b)
implies that
c
is bluish, because otherwise
\left\{{a,c}\right\}
is a cut enhancer. Thus
\left\{{a}\right\}
is an absolute tail reducer by
Lemma

3.5
.

Lemma 3.7
.

For every maximum cut
(A,B)
of a subcubic graph
G
, if
a
is a red vertex of degree
3
,
b
is a blue vertex of degree
3
,
c
is a reddish vertex,
a\sim b
, and
b\sim c
, then
c
is of degree
3
in
G
, or
\left\{{a,c}\right\}
is an absolute tail reducer.

Proof.

Proposition

2.11
implies that
\left\{{a,b}\right\}
is a cut preserver. Flip the cut preserver
\left\{{a,b}\right\}
. Since
c
becomes red,
Lemma

3.6
implies that
c
is of degree
3
, or
\left\{{c}\right\}
becomes an absolute tail reducer. In the latter case,
\left\{{a,c}\right\}
is an absolute tail reducer with respect to
(A,B)
by
Lemma

2.12
and the remark following its proof on
Remark

Remark
.

We shall repeatedly use the following results on induced paths and induced even cycles in
M
.

Lemma 3.8
.

For every maximum cut
(A,B)
of a subcubic graph
G
, if the underlying multigraph contains an induced path
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{n}
, then
G
contains an induced path
a_{1}b_{1}\dots a_{n}b_{n}
such that
\alpha_{1}=a_{1}b_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}=a_{n}b_{n}
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha_{1}=a_{1}b_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}=a_{2}b_{2}
be such that
G[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}]
consists of the single edge
b_{1}a_{2}
. To find the next
a_{i},b_{i}
, let
\alpha_{i}=a_{i}b_{i}
such that either
a_{i}\sim a_{i-1}
or
a_{i}\sim b_{i-1}
.
Lemma

3.3
implies that
a_{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\sim$\cr$\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin={c}]{50.0}{$\displaystyle-$}$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\sim$\cr$\textstyle\rotatebox[origin={c}]{50.0}{$\textstyle-$}$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\sim$\cr$\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin={c}]{50.0}{$\scriptstyle-$}$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin={c}]{50.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}$\cr}}}}a_{i-1}
because
a_{i-1}\sim b_{i-2}
, and so
a_{i}\sim b_{i-1}
.
∎

Lemma 3.9
.

For every maximum cut
(A,B)
of a subcubic graph
G
, if the underlying multigraph contains an induced even cycle
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{2n}
, then
G
contains an induced cycle
a_{1}b_{1}\dots a_{2n}b_{2n}
such that
\alpha_{1}=a_{1}b_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}=a_{n}b_{n}
.

Proof.

We find
a_{1},b_{1},\dots,a_{2n},b_{2n}
the same way as in the proof of
Lemma

3.8
. Finally,
Lemma

3.3
implies that
a_{2n}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\sim$\cr$\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin={c}]{50.0}{$\displaystyle-$}$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\sim$\cr$\textstyle\rotatebox[origin={c}]{50.0}{$\textstyle-$}$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\sim$\cr$\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin={c}]{50.0}{$\scriptstyle-$}$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin={c}]{50.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}$\cr}}}}a_{1}
and
a_{2n}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\sim$\cr$\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin={c}]{50.0}{$\displaystyle-$}$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\sim$\cr$\textstyle\rotatebox[origin={c}]{50.0}{$\textstyle-$}$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\sim$\cr$\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin={c}]{50.0}{$\scriptstyle-$}$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin={c}]{50.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}$\cr}}}}b_{1}
because
a_{2n}\sim b_{2n-1}
, and so either
b_{2n}\sim a_{1}
or
b_{2n}\sim b_{1}
, and furthermore
Lemma

3.3
implies that
b_{2n}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\sim$\cr$\displaystyle\rotatebox[origin={c}]{50.0}{$\displaystyle-$}$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\sim$\cr$\textstyle\rotatebox[origin={c}]{50.0}{$\textstyle-$}$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\sim$\cr$\scriptstyle\rotatebox[origin={c}]{50.0}{$\scriptstyle-$}$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\rotatebox[origin={c}]{50.0}{$\scriptscriptstyle-$}$\cr}}}}b_{1}
because
b_{1}\sim a_{2}
, and so
b_{2n}\sim a_{1}
.
∎

Proposition 4.1
.

For every maximum cut
(A,B)
of a subcubic graph
G
, if either one of the graphs in
Figure

6
is an induced subgraph
F
of
G
, or the graph in
Figure

7(a)
is an induced subgraph
F
of
G
satisfying the indicated degree constraint, then
A\cap V(F)
is a positive tail reducer.

Proof.

Let
D=A\cap V(F)
. Suppose that
F
is one of the graphs in
Figure

6
. Each reddish vertex has degree
3
in
F
, each red vertex has two neighbors in
B\cap V(F)
, and each blue vertex has its blue neighbor in
F
. By subcubicity and
Proposition

2.3
, no edge of
G[D\cup B]
joins
V(F)
to its complement. Thus
F
is the connected component of
G[D\cup B]
containing
D
. The inequality
t_{F}^{+}<\lvert D\rvert
is verified in
Appendix

A
.

Finally suppose that
F
is the graph in
Figure

7(a)
. The degree constraint implies, by the same argument, that
F
is the connected component of
G[D\cup B]
containing
D
. Moreover,
F
is obtained from the graph
F^{\prime}
in
Figure

6(m)
by deleting vertex
c
. By the computation in
Appendix

A
and the Cauchy interlacing theorem,
t_{F}^{+}\leq t_{F^{\prime}}^{+}<2=\lvert D\rvert
.
∎

Throughout the rest of this section, we assume that
(A,B)
is a maximum cut of a subcubic graph
G
, and
M
is the underlying multigraph of
G
with respect to
(A,B)
. When the conclusion of a result in this section is of the form “a subset of
U
is a positive tail reducer” for a specific vertex subset
U
of
G
, in view of
Lemma

3.6
, we always assume from the start of the proof that every red or blue vertex in
U
is of degree
3
.

Roughly speaking, we establish, through the next three lemmas, that every connected component of
M
is a path or a cycle of even length if
G
lacks positive tail reducers.

Lemma 4.2
.

If
M
contains multiple edges between
\alpha
and
\beta
, then a subset of
\alpha\cup\beta
is a positive tail reducer.

Proof.

Let
\alpha=ab
and
\beta=cd
. In view of
Proposition

2.14
, we may assume that
ab
is red, and
cd
is blue up to symmetry.

(1)

If
M
contains
4
edges between
ab
and
cd
, then
\left\{{a,b}\right\}
is a positive tail reducer by
Figure

6(a)
.
a
b
c
d

(2)

Suppose
M
contains
3
edges between
ab
and
cd
. We may assume that
a\sim c,a\sim d
, and
b\sim d
up to symmetry. Let
e
be the third neighbor of
b
.
Lemma

3.3
implies that
e
is bluish, and so
\left\{{a,b}\right\}
is a positive tail reducer by
Figure

6(d)
.
a
b
c
d
e

(3)

Suppose
M
contains
2
edges between
ab
and
cd
.

(3.1)

Suppose the
2
edges between
ab
and
cd
are vertex-disjoint. We may assume that
a\sim c
and
b\sim d
up to symmetry.
Lemma

3.3
implies that the third neighbor of
a
and that of
b
are bluish. Depending on whether
a
and
b
share a bluish neighbor, there are two possibilities. In either case,
\left\{{a,b}\right\}
is a positive tail reducer by
Figures

6(f)
and
6(i)
.
a
b
c
d
e
a
b
c
d
e
f

(3.2)

Suppose the
2
edges between
ab
and
cd
share a vertex. We may assume that
a\sim c
and
a\sim d
up to symmetry.
Lemma

3.3
implies that the third neighbor of
c
and that of
d
are reddish. Depending on whether
c
and
d
share a reddish neighbor, there are two possibilities. In either case,
\left\{{c,d}\right\}
is a positive tail reducer by
Figures

6(e)
and
6(j)
.

Lemma 4.3
.

If
\alpha
is a vertex of
M
, then
\alpha
is of degree at most
2
in
M
, or a subset of
G(\alpha,1)
is a positive tail reducer.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\alpha=ab
is red.
Lemma

3.3
implies that neither
a
nor
b
can be adjacent to two blue edges in
G
. If
\alpha
is of degree at least
3
in
M
, then
M
contains multiple edges between
\alpha
and its neighbor
\beta
, and so a subset of
\alpha\cup\beta
is a positive tail reducer by
Lemma

4.2
. Finally, notice that the distance between every vertex of
\beta
and
\alpha
is
1
.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

If
\alpha
is an isolated vertex of
M
, then a vertex subset of
\alpha
is a positive tail reducer.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\alpha=ab
is red. Since
ab
is isolated in
M
, the other neighbors of
a
and
b
are bluish. Depending on how these bluish neighbors overlap, there are three possibilities. In each case,
\left\{{a,b}\right\}
is a positive tail reducer by
Figures

6(b)
,
6(h)
and

In the remaining proofs of this section, we shall obtain subsets
C
and
D
of
U
, for a specific vertex subset
U
of
G
, such that
C
is a cut preserver, and
D
is a positive tail reducer with respect to
(A\oplus C,B\oplus C)
. We always automatically apply
Lemma

2.12
to obtain a subset of
U
that is a positive tail reducer with respect to
(A,B)
.

Lemma 4.5
.

If
M(\alpha,4)
is a path on at least
5
vertices, or a cycle on at least
6
vertices, then a subset of
G(\alpha,8)
is a positive tail reducer.

Proof.

Observe that
M(\alpha,4)
contains an induced path on
5
vertices, say
ab,cd,ef,gh,ij
, such that
\alpha\in\left\{{ab,cd,ef,gh,ij}\right\}
. In view of
Lemma

3.8
, we may assume that
abcdefghij
is an induced path in
G
, and moreover we may assume that
ab,ef,ij
are red, and
cd,gh
are blue. Let
k
be the third neighbor of
e
.
Lemma

3.3
implies that
k
is bluish.

implies that

Lemma 4.6
.

If
M
contains a path
\alpha\beta\gamma\delta\epsilon
as a subgraph, then a subset of
G(\gamma,9)
is a positive tail reducer.

Proof.

(1)

If
M(\gamma,4)
contains multiple edges, then a subset of
G(\gamma,8)
is a positive tail reducer by
Lemma

4.2
.

(2)

If
M(\gamma,4)
is a simple graph of maximum degree at least
3
, then a subset of
G(\gamma,9)
is a positive tail reducer by
Lemma

4.3
.

(3)

If
M(\gamma,4)
is a simple graph of maximum degree at most
2
, then
M(\gamma,4)
must be a path on at least
5
vertices, or any even cycle on at least
6
vertices, and so a subset of
G(\gamma,8)
by
Lemma

4.5
. ∎

Lemma 4.7
.

If
M(\alpha,4)
is a cycle on
4
vertices, then a subset of
G(\alpha,3)
is a positive tail reducer.

Proof.

Suppose that
M(\alpha,4)
is the cycle
ab,cd,ef,gh
such that
\alpha\in\left\{{ab,cd,ef,gh}\right\}
. In view of
Lemma

3.9
, we may assume that
abcdefgh
is an induced cycle in
G
, and moreover we may assume that
ab,ef
are red, and
cd,gh
are blue.
a

e
f
c
d
g
h

Lemma

3.3
implies that the third neighbors of
a,b,e,f
are all bluish.
Figure

implies that
a
and
e
do not share a bluish neighbor because otherwise
\left\{{a,d,e,h,i}\right\}
is a cut enhancer, where
i
is the shared bluish neighbor of
a
and
e
. Similarly
b
and
f
do not share a bluish neighbor.

(1)

If
b
and
e
share a bluish neighbor, then
\left\{{b,e}\right\}
is a positive tail reducer by
Figure

6(c)
.
a

e
f

g

(2)

Suppose

and

a
and
f
. Depending on how the bluish neighbors of
a,b,e,f
overlap, there are three possibilities up to symmetry. In each case,
\left\{{a,b,e,f}\right\}
is a positive tail reducer by

6(v)
,

and

is

, and it is the furthest distance between any vertex in a positive tail reducer and
\alpha\in\left\{{ab,cd,ef,gh}\right\}
.

Behold, our first foray into the depths: a 7-level case analysis. Let’s dive in!

Finally, notice that the distance between a vertex in
G(cd,9)
and
gh
is at most

in
(3.4.3.1.3.3.1)
, and it is the furthest distance possible between any vertex in a cut preserver or a positive tail reducer and
\alpha\in\left\{{ab,cd,ef,gh}\right\}
.
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

If
M
contains a path
\alpha\beta\gamma\delta
as a subgraph, then a subset of
G(\beta,12)
is a positive tail reducer.

Proof.

(1)

If
M(\beta,4)
contains multiple edges, then a subset of
G(\beta,8)
is a positive tail reducer by
Lemma

4.2
.

(2)

If
M(\beta,4)
is a simple graph of maximum degree at least
3
, then a subset of
G(\beta,9)
is a positive tail reducer by
Lemma

4.3
.

(3)

If
M(\beta,4)
is a simple graph of maximum degree at most
2
, then
M(\beta,4)
must be a path on at least
4
vertices, or an even cycle (on at least
4
vertices), and so a subset of
G(\beta,12)
is a positive tail reducer by
Lemmas

4.5
,
4.7
and
4.8
. ∎

Lo and behold, we now descend into yet another 7-level case analysis. May your coffee be strong and your patience stronger.

Lemma 4.11
.

If
M
contains a path
\alpha\beta\gamma
as a subgraph, then a subset of
G(\beta,14)
is a positive tail reducer.

Proof.

(1)

If
M(\beta,4)
contains multiple edges, then a subset of
G(\beta,8)
is a positive tail reducer by
Lemma

4.2
.

(2)

If
M(\beta,4)
is a simple graph of maximum degree at least
3
, then a subset of
G(\beta,9)
is a positive tail reducer by
Lemma

4.3
.

(3)

If
M(\beta,4)
is a simple graph of maximum degree at most
2
, then
M(\beta,4)
must be a path on at least
3
vertices, or an even cycle (on at least
4
vertices), and so a subset of
G(\beta,14)
is a positive tail reducer by
Lemmas

4.5
,
4.7
,
4.8
and
4.10
. ∎

Lemma 4.12
.

is a path on

vertices, then a subset of

is a positive tail reducer.

and

because otherwise

or

is an absolute tail reducer by
Lemma

3.7
.

Proof of
Lemma

2.7
.

Let
\alpha
be a vertex of
M
.

(1)

If
M(\alpha,4)
contains multiple edges, then a subset of
G(\alpha,8)
is a positive tail reducer by
Lemma

4.2
.

(2)

If
M(\alpha,4)
is a simple graph of maximum degree at least
3
, then a subset of
G(\alpha,9)
is a positive tail reducer by
Lemma

4.3
.

(3)

If
M(\alpha,4)
is a simple graph of maximum degree at most
2
, then
M(\alpha,4)
must be an isolated vertex, a path (on at least
2
vertices), or an even cycle (on at least
4
vertices), and so a subset of
G(\alpha,16)
is a positive tail reducer by
Lemmas

4.4
,
4.5
,
4.7
,
4.8
,
4.10
and
4.12
. ∎

Figure 10

5
Negative tail reducers

We collect all the negative tail reducers ever needed in the following proposition.

Proposition 5.1
.

For every maximum cut
(A,B)
of a subcubic graph
G
, if either one of the graphs in
Figure

8
is an induced subgraph
F
of
G
, or one of the graphs in
Figure

10
is an induced subgraph
F
of
G
satisfying the indicated degree constraints, then
A\cap V(F)
is a negative tail reducer.

Proof.

Let
D=A\cap V(F)
. Suppose that
F
is one of the graphs in
Figure

8
. Each reddish vertex has degree
3
in
F
, each red vertex has two neighbors in
B\cap V(F)
, and each blue vertex has its blue neighbor in
F
. By subcubicity and
Proposition

2.3
, no edge of
G[D\cup B]
joins
V(F)
to its complement. Thus
F
is the connected component of
G[D\cup B]
containing
D
. The inequality
t_{F}^{-}<\lvert D\rvert
is verified in
Appendix

A
.

Finally suppose that
F
is one of the graphs in
Figure

10
. The displayed colors and degree constraints imply, by the same argument, that
F
is the connected component of
G[D\cup B]
containing
D
. Ignoring colors and degree constraints,
F
is obtained from the corresponding graph
F^{\prime}
in
Figure

8
by deleting vertices, possibly none, in
B
. By the computation in
Appendix

A
and the Cauchy interlacing theorem,
t_{F}^{-}\leq t_{F^{\prime}}^{-}<\lvert A\cap V(F^{\prime})\rvert=\lvert D\rvert
.
∎

Just like in
Section

4
, throughout the rest of this section, we assume that
(A,B)
is a maximum cut of a subcubic graph
G
, and
M
is the underlying multigraph of
G
with respect to
(A,B)
. When the conclusion of a result in this section is of the form “a subset of
U
is a negative tail reducer” for a specific vertex subset
U
of
G
, in view of
Lemma

3.6
, we always assume from the start of the proof that every red or blue vertex in
U
is of degree
3
.

We establish a series of lemmas in parallel to those in
Section

4
. In their proofs, we often obtain subsets
C
and
D
of
U
, for a specific vertex subset
U
of
G
, such that
C
is a cut preserver, and
D
is a negative tail reducer with respect to
(A\oplus C,B\oplus C)
. We always automatically apply
Lemma

2.12
to obtain a subset of
U
that is a negative tail reducer with respect to
(A,B)
.

Lemma 5.2
.

If
M
contains multiple edges between
\alpha
and
\beta
, then a subset of
G(\alpha,2)
is a negative tail reducer.

Proof.

Let
\alpha=ab
and
\beta=cd
. In view of
Proposition

2.14
, we may assume that
ab
is red, and
cd
is blue up to symmetry.

(1)

Suppose that the edges between
\left\{{a,b}\right\}
and
\left\{{c,d}\right\}
do not form a matching in
G
. We may assume that
a\sim c
and
a\sim d
up to symmetry. Then
\left\{{a}\right\}
is a negative tail reducer by
Figure

8(a)
.
a
b
c
d

(2)

and

.

implies that

is bluish.

Lemma 5.3
.

If
\alpha
is a vertex of
M
, then
\alpha
is of degree at most
2
in
M
, or a subset of
G(\alpha,2)
is a negative tail reducer.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\alpha=ab
is red.
Lemma

3.3
implies that neither
a
nor
b
can be adjacent to two blue edges in
G
. If
\alpha
is of degree at least
3
in
M
, then
M
contains multiple edges between
\alpha
and its neighbor
\beta
, and so a subset of
G(\alpha,2)
is a negative tail reducer by
Lemma

5.2
. Finally, notice that the distance between a vertex of
G(\alpha,2)
and
\alpha
is at most
2
.
∎

The last three lemmas establish that every connected component of

lacks negative tail reducers. We introduce the following versatile ingredient on pentagons.

Finally, notice that every negative tail reducer above is a subset of
\left\{{a,b,c}\right\}
.
∎

As an application of

, we build the following tool to deal with paths and cycles.

Finally, notice that the distance between a vertex in
G(mn,4)
and
gh
is at most
10
in
(3.4.4.3.1)
, and it is the furthest distance possible between any vertex in a cut preserver or a negative tail reducer and
\alpha\in\left\{{ab,cd,ef,gh}\right\}
.
∎

Lemma 5.10
.

If
M
contains a path
\alpha\beta\gamma\delta
as a subgraph, then a subset of
G(\beta,10)
is a negative tail reducer.

Proof.

(1)

If
M(\beta,4)
contains multiple edges, then a subset of
G(\beta,10)
is a negative tail reducer by
Lemma

5.2
.

(2)

If
M(\beta,4)
is a simple graph of maximum degree at least
3
, then a subset of
G(\beta,10)
is a negative tail reducer by
Lemma

5.3
.

(3)

If
M(\beta,4)
is a simple graph of maximum degree at most
2
, then
M(\beta,4)
must be a path on at least
4
vertices, or an even cycle (on at least
4
vertices), and so a subset of
G(\beta,10)
is a negative tail reducer by

Lemma 5.12
.

If
M
contains a path
\alpha\beta\gamma
as a subgraph, then a subset of
G(\beta,12)
is a negative tail reducer.

Proof.

(1)

If
M(\beta,4)
contains multiple edges, then a subset of
G(\beta,10)
is a negative tail reducer by
Lemma

5.2
.

(2)

If
M(\beta,4)
is a simple graph of maximum degree at least
3
, then a subset of
G(\beta,10)
is a negative tail reducer by
Lemma

5.3
.

(3)

If
M(\beta,4)
is a simple graph of maximum degree at most
2
, then
M(\beta,4)
must be a path on at least
3
vertices, or an even cycle (on at least
4
vertices), and so a subset of
G(\beta,12)
is a negative tail reducer by
Lemmas

5.7
,
5.8
,
5.9
and
5.11
. ∎

Proof of
Lemma

2.8
.

Let
\alpha
be a vertex of
M
.

(1)

contains multiple edges, then a subset of
G(\alpha,10)
is a negative tail reducer by
Lemma

5.2
.

(2)

If
M(\alpha,4)
is a simple graph of maximum degree at least
3
, then a subset of
G(\alpha,10)
is a negative tail reducer by
Lemma

5.3
.

(3)

If
M(\alpha,4)
is a simple graph of maximum degree at most
2
, then
M(\alpha,4)
must be an isolated vertex, a path (on at least
2
vertices), or an even cycle (on at least
4
vertices), and so a subset of
G(\alpha,14)
is a negative tail reducer by
Lemmas

5.4
,
5.7
,
5.8
,
5.9
,
5.11
and
5.13
. ∎

6
Positive fraction

To prove the positive fraction result
Theorem

1.2
for connected subcubic graphs, we follow Mohar’s strategy in
[
14
]
to combine ‘local’ tail reducers.

To make use of
Lemma

2.6
when the graph is not bipartite, we introduce the following result so that
Lemma

2.6
is applicable as long as the graph is ‘locally’ bipartite.

Lemma 6.1
.

For every vertex subset
A
of a graph
G
, and every induced subgraph
H
of
G
, if all the neighbors of
V(H)\setminus A
in
G
are in
A
, then

t_{H}^{\pm}(A^{\prime})-t_{H}^{\pm}(A^{\prime}\setminus C)=t_{G}^{\pm}(A)-t_{G}^{\pm}(A\setminus C)

for every vertex subset
C
of
H
, where
A^{\prime}=V(H)\cap A
.

Proof.

We shall only prove the statement for the positive tail estimate. The proof for the negative tail estimate is similar. Set
B=V(G)\setminus A
and
B^{\prime}=V(H)\setminus A
, and fix a vertex subset
C
of
H
. Based on the definition of positive tail estimates, it suffices to show that

t_{H[B^{\prime}\cup C]}^{+}-t_{H[B^{\prime}]}^{+}=t_{G[B\cup C]}^{+}-t_{G[B]}^{+}.

Since all the neighbors of
V(H)\setminus A
in
G
are in
A
, all the connected components of
H[B^{\prime}]
are trivial, and moreover the nontrivial connected components of
H[B^{\prime}\cup C]
, denoted by
H_{1},\dots,H_{k}
, are precisely the nontrivial connected components of
G[B\cup C]
that have not appeared in
G[B]
. Therefore both sides of the equation are equal to
t^{+}_{H_{1}}+\dots+t^{+}_{H_{k}}
.
∎

The next result lets us combine ‘local’ tail reducers.

Lemma 6.2
.

For every vertex subset
A
of a graph
G
, and every family of vertex subsets
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
of
G
, if each connected component of
G-A
has diameter at most
d
, and
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
are pairwise at distance at least
d+3
, then

t^{\pm}_{G}(A)-t^{\pm}_{G}\left(A\setminus\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}C_{i}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}t^{\pm}_{G}(A)-t^{\pm}_{G}(A\setminus C_{i}).

Proof.

We shall only prove the statement for the positive tail estimate. Set
C=C_{1}\cup\dots\cup C_{k}
, and
B=V(G)\setminus A
. It suffices to show that

t^{+}_{G[B\cup C]}-t^{+}_{G[B]}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}t^{+}_{G[B\cup C_{i}]}-t^{+}_{G[B]}.

Let
\mathcal{H}
(respectively,
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
) be the set of the connected components of
G[B]
(respectively,
G[B\cup C]
) that are within distance
1
from
C
in
G
. Note that

t^{+}_{G[B\cup C]}-t^{+}_{G[B]}=\lvert A\cap C\rvert+\sum_{H\in\mathcal{H}}t^{+}_{H}-\sum_{H^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}t^{+}_{H^{\prime}}.

For each
i\in\left\{{1,\dots,k}\right\}
, let
\mathcal{H}_{i}
(respectively,
\mathcal{H}_{i}^{\prime}
) be the set of the connected components of
G[B]
(respectively,
G[B\cup C_{i}]
) that are within distance
1
from
C_{i}
in
G
. Note that

t^{\pm}_{G}(A)-t^{\pm}_{G}(A\setminus C_{i})=\lvert A\cap C_{i}\rvert+t^{+}_{G[B]}-t^{+}_{G[B\cup C_{i}]}=\lvert A\cap C_{i}\rvert+\sum_{H\in{\mathcal{H}_{i}}}t^{+}_{H}-\sum_{H^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}_{i}^{\prime}}t^{+}_{H^{\prime}}.

Finally, since
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
are pairwise at distance at least
d+3
, we know that
\lvert A\cap C\rvert=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lvert A\cap C_{i}\rvert
, and moreover
\mathcal{H}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{H}_{k}
(respectively,
\mathcal{H}_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\mathcal{H}_{k}^{\prime}
) form a partition of
\mathcal{H}
(respectively,
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
).
∎

We finally have all the ingredients for the proof of our second main theorem.

Proof of
Theorem

1.2
.

With hindsight, we choose
\eta=2^{-56}
. Let
G
be a connected subcubic graph on
n
vertices, and let
(A,B)
be a maximum cut of
G
.

We may assume that no subgraph of
G
can be isomorphic to the Heawood graph. Indeed, suppose for a moment that a subgraph of
G
is isomorphic to the Heawood graph. Since
G
is connected subcubic and the Heawood graph is
3
-regular, it must be the case that
G
itself is isomorphic to the Heawood graph. In particular,
n=14
, in which case the theorem is vacuously true because
[\tfrac{1}{2}(1-\eta)(n+1),\tfrac{1}{2}(1+\eta)(n+1)]
contains no integers.

Claim 1
.

There exist vertices
v_{1},\dots,v_{j}
of
G
and edges
\alpha_{j+1},\dots,\alpha_{k}
of
G[A]\cup G[B]
such that
v_{1},\dots,v_{j},\alpha_{j+1},\dots,\alpha_{k}
are pairwise at distance at least
38
in
G
, each
G(v_{i},18)
is a subgraph of the bipartite graph
G[A,B]
for
i\in\left\{{1,\dots,j}\right\}
, and
k\geq\eta n
.

Proof of
Claim

1
.

Starting with
V_{0}=V(G)
, recursively we pick an edge
\alpha_{i}
(if any) of
G[A\cap V_{i-1}]\cup G[B\cap V_{i-1}]
, and then we obtain
V_{i}
from
V_{i-1}
by removing the vertices within distance
37
from
\alpha_{i}
in
G
. Let
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{j}
be the edges we have picked. Set
U:=V(G)\setminus V_{j}
, and let
U^{\prime}
be the set of vertices within distance
17
from
U
in
G
.

Starting with
W_{0}=V(G)\setminus U^{\prime}
, we now recursively pick a vertex
v_{i}
of
G[W_{i-1}]
, and then we obtain
W_{i}
from
W_{i-1}
by removing the vertices within distance
38
from
v_{i}
in
G
. Let
v_{1},\dots,v_{k}
be the vertices we have picked.

Clearly,
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{j},v_{1},\dots,v_{k}
are pairwise at distance at least
37
in
G
. Since
G-U
is a subgraph of the bipartite graph
G[A,B]
, and each
v_{i}
is at distance more than
18
from
U
, we know that each
G(v_{i},17)
is a subgraph of
G[A,B]
.

Finally, we are left to prove that
j+k\geq\eta n
. Because
\lvert U^{\prime}\rvert\leq\lvert G(\alpha_{1},37+17)\rvert+\dots+\lvert G(\alpha_{j},37+17)\rvert\leq 2^{56}j
, and
\lvert W_{0}\rvert\leq\lvert G(v_{1},37)\rvert+\dots+\lvert G(v_{k},37)\rvert\leq 2^{39}k
, we know that
n=\lvert U^{\prime}\rvert+\lvert W_{0}\rvert\leq 2^{56}j+2^{39}k
, and so
j+k\geq 2^{-56}n
.
∎

Claim 2
.

There exist vertex subsets
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
of
G
such that each
C_{i}
decreases
t^{+}_{G}(A)
or
t^{+}_{G}(B)
, and
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
are pairwise at distance at least
4
in
G
.

Proof of
Claim

2
.

Let
v_{1},\dots,v_{j},\alpha_{j+1},\dots,\alpha_{k}
be the vertices and edges as in
Claim

1
.

For
i\in\left\{{1,\dots,j}\right\}
, since
G(v_{i},17)
is not the Heawood graph, we can apply
Lemma

2.6
to the graph
G(v_{i},17)
and the vertex
v_{i}
to obtain a vertex subset
C_{i}
of
G(v_{i},17)
such that
C_{i}
decreases
t^{+}_{G(v_{i},17)}(A)
or
t^{+}_{G(v_{i},17)}(B)
. Since
G(v_{i},18)
is bipartite, we know from
Lemma

6.1
that
C_{i}
also decreases
t^{+}_{G}(A)
or
t^{+}_{G}(B)
.

For
i\in\left\{{j+1,\dots,k}\right\}
, since
(A,B)
is a maximum cut, we can apply
Lemma

2.7
to the graph
G
, and the edge
\alpha_{i}
to obtain a positive tail reducer
C_{i}
that is a subset of
G(\alpha_{i},16)
.

Since
v_{1},\dots,v_{j},\alpha_{j+1},\dots,\alpha_{k}
are pairwise at distance at least
38
, the vertex subsets
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
are pairwise at distance at least
4
in
G
.
∎

Let
C_{1},\dots,C_{k}
be the vertex subsets as in
Claim

2
. Set
I_{A}:=\left\{{i}\colon{C_{i}\text{ decreases }t^{+}_{G}(A)}\right\}
and
I_{B}=\left\{{i}\colon{C_{i}\text{ decreases }t^{+}_{G}(B)}\right\}
. From
Proposition

2.3
, we know that each connected component of
G[A]\cup G[B]
has diameter at most
1
, and moreover
t^{+}_{G}(A)=\lvert A\rvert
and
t^{+}_{G}(B)=\lvert B\rvert
. We can apply
Lemma

6.2
separately to
\left\{{C_{i}}\colon{i\in I_{A}}\right\}
and
\left\{{C_{i}}\colon{i\in I_{B}}\right\}
to obtain

t_{G}^{+}\left(A\setminus\bigcup_{i\in I_{A}}C_{i}\right)\leq\lvert A\rvert-\lvert I_{A}\rvert\text{ and }t_{G}^{+}\left(B\setminus\bigcup_{i\in I_{B}}C_{i}\right)\leq\lvert B\rvert-\lvert I_{B}\rvert,

which implies via
Lemma

2.2
that

t^{+}_{G}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}(\lvert A\rvert-\lvert I_{A}\rvert+\lvert B\rvert-\lvert I_{B}\rvert)\leq\tfrac{1}{2}(n-k)\leq\tfrac{1}{2}(1-\eta)n<\tfrac{1}{2}(1-\eta)(n+1).

Finally, one can also show that
t^{-}_{G}<\tfrac{1}{2}(1-\eta)(n+1)
through a symmetric argument.
∎

7
Further remarks

In this paper, we showed that the median eigenvalues of every connected graph of maximum degree at most three, except for the Heawood graph, are at most
1
in absolute value. This result contributes to a broader line of research on bounding the median eigenvalues of graphs of bounded maximum degree.

A significant related result is due to Mohar and Tayfeh-Rezaie
[
15
]
, who proved that for every
d\geq 3
, the median eigenvalues of every connected
bipartite
graph
G
of maximum degree
d
are at most
\sqrt{d-2}
in absolute value, unless
G
is the incidence graph of a projective plane of order
d-1
, in which case the median eigenvalues are equal to
\pm\sqrt{d-1}
. This generalizes an earlier result of Mohar
[
14
]
and raises the question of optimality of the bound
\sqrt{d-2}
.

Problem 7.1
.

For every
d\geq 3
and every
\varepsilon>0
, are there infinitely many connected bipartite graphs
G
of maximum degree
d
whose median eigenvalues have absolute value greater than
\sqrt{d-2}-\varepsilon
?

For
d=3
, this question was resolved positively by a construction of Guo and Mohar
[
6
]
: they constructed an infinite family of connected bipartite cubic graphs, known as the Guo–Mohar graphs, whose median eigenvalues are
-1
and
+1
. Recent work
[
7
,
9
]
gives a classification of all connected subcubic graphs whose median eigenvalues satisfy
\lambda_{\ell}=-1
and
\lambda_{h}=+1
: they are the Guo–Mohar graphs and nine of the thirteen sporadic graphs from
[
7
]
, together with the Huang–Jiang graphs and seven of the eight sporadic graphs from
[
9
]
. We point out that the Kollár–Sarnak graphs, first introduced in
[
10
]
and identified in
[
7
]
as the other infinite family of connected cubic graphs with no eigenvalues in
(-1,1)
, have both median eigenvalues equal to
-1
. We refer the reader to
[
9
, Section 6.3]
for further discussion.

However,
Problem

7.1
remains wide open for
d\geq 4
. Mohar and Tayfeh-Rezaie also observed that the incidence graph of a symmetric
2
-design with parameters
(v,d,2)
, also known as a biplane of order
d-2
, would provide a
d
-regular bipartite graph of order
2v
whose median eigenvalues are exactly
\pm\sqrt{d-2}
, where
v=d(d-1)/2+1
. The existence of infinitely many biplanes is a long-standing open problem in design theory (see
[
8
, Chapter 15.8]
).

If we remove the bipartite constraint, earlier work by Mohar
[
13
]
established that for every
d\geq 3
, the median eigenvalues of every connected graph of maximum degree
d
are at most
\sqrt{d}
in absolute value, and specifically for
d=3
, this bound can be improved to
\sqrt{d-1}
. We reiterate the conjecture that the same improvement should hold for every
d\geq 4
.

Very recently, Acharya, Jiang and Zhang
[
1
]
resolved Mohar’s conjecture for all but finitely many values of
d
. In particular, they proved that the median eigenvalues of any graph of maximum degree
d
are bounded above by
\sqrt{d-1}
. They also proved the corresponding lower bound
-\sqrt{d-1}
in three cases: when the graph is triangle-free, when
d-1
is a perfect square, or when
d\geq 75
.

Appendix A
Computer-assisted proofs

Proof of
Propositions

4.1
and
5.1
.

Recall Sylvester’s law of inertia: if
A
is a symmetric matrix, for any invertible matrix
S
, the number of positive eigenvalues, also known as
the positive index of inertia
, of
D=SAS^{\intercal}
is constant. This result is particularly useful when
D
is diagonal, as the positive index of inertia is equal to the number of positive diagonal entries of
D
.

Note that the positive and negative tails of
G
are equal to the positive index of inertia of
A_{G}-I
and
-A_{G}-I
respectively. Therefore the core of our algorithm needs to compute the positive index of inertia of a symmetric matrix
A
with rational entries. Given a symmetric matrix
A
of order
n
with rational entries, we compute its positive index of inertia
p
through the
LDL^{\intercal}
decomposition.

The algorithm initializes a counter
p
to track positive diagonal elements and processes each row
i
from
1
to
n
. For each row
i
, it identifies the first non-zero element
A_{ij}
. If no such
j
exists, the algorithm continues to the next row. Otherwise, it ensures the diagonal element
A_{ii}
is non-zero by adding row and column
j
to row and column
i
if necessary. If
A_{ii}
is still zero after these operations, which happens if and only if
A_{ii}=0
and
A_{jj}=-2A_{ij}
, the algorithm swaps row
i
and row
j
, as well as column
i
and column
j
. After swapping,
A_{ii}
becomes
A_{jj}
, which is nonzero. If
A_{ii}>0
,
p
is incremented. The algorithm then eliminates off-diagonal elements in column
i
by subtracting a multiple of row
i
from subsequent rows, and eliminates off-diagonal elements in row
i
by subtracting a multiple of column
i
from subsequent columns. Finally, it returns
p
. The pseudocode is described in
Algorithm

1
.

1

p\leftarrow 0
;

2

for

i\leftarrow 1

to

n

do

3

j\leftarrow
the smallest
j\geq i
such that
A_{ij}\neq 0
;

4

if

j
does not exist

then

5

Continue to the next iteration;

6

end if

7

if

i<j

then

8

A_{i*}\leftarrow A_{i*}+A_{j*}
;
/*
Add row
j
to row
i
*/

9

A_{*i}\leftarrow A_{*i}+A_{*j}
;
/*
Add col
j
to col
i
*/

10

if

A_{ii}=0

then

11

A_{i*},A_{j*}\leftarrow A_{j*},A_{i*}
;
/*
Swap row
i
and row
j
*/

12

A_{*i},A_{*j}\leftarrow A_{*j},A_{*i}
;
/*
Swap col
i
and col
j
*/

13

end if

14

end if

15

if

A_{ii}>0

then

16

p\leftarrow p+1
;

17

end if

18

for

j\leftarrow i+1

to

n

do

19

A_{j*}\leftarrow A_{j*}-(A_{ji}/A_{ii})A_{i*}
;
/*
Subtract a multiple of row
i
from row
j
*/

20

A_{*j}\leftarrow A_{*j}-(A_{ij}/A_{ii})A_{*i}
;
/*
Subtract a multiple of col
i
from col
j
*/

21

end for

22

end for

23

return

p

Algorithm 1

The
LDL^{\intercal}
decomposition.

Coming back to the description of the main program, we describe the input and output format.

Input.

Each line represents a graph by two strings. The first string is the label of the graph, which ends with
+z
or
-z
. Here the
+
or
-
sign indicates whether we would like to compute positive or negative tail, and the number
z
stands for the number of red or reddish vertices. The second string is of the form
v[1]v[2]...v[2e-1]v[2e]
, which lists the edges.

Output.

For each graph
F
, output one line containing
x y
, where
x
is the label of
F
, which ends with
+z
or
-z
, and
y
is the positive or negative tail of
F
depending on the label.

Our implementation is straightforward. We avoid floating-point errors by representing every number as a rational number. The actual code, written in Ruby, is available as the ancillary file
anc/tails.rb
in the arXiv version of this paper. Since the output has
z
greater than
y
on each line, we obtain a proof of
Propositions

4.1
and
5.1
. We provide the input in
Table

1
for the convenience of anyone who wants to program independently, and we also provide the output in
Table

2
for cross-checking.

Appendix B
Lean formalization

The formalization covers
Lemmas

2.7
and
2.8
and all lemmas in
Sections

4
and
5
needed for their proofs. Because our aim is to verify the case analysis rather than formalize all surrounding infrastructure, we make two simplifications. First, instead of formalizing the maximum cut
(A,B)
, we assume that the edges of
G[A]\cup G[B]
form a matching, as established by
Proposition

2.3
; we call such a cut a
matching cut
. We also assume that the cut admits none of the cut enhancers in
Figures

3
and
4
. Second, the formalized statements assert the existence of one of the tail reducers specified in
Figures

6
,
7
,
8
and
10
, but omit its distance bound.

More precisely, the positive-tail and negative-tail case analyses are formalized as the following theorem.

Theorem B.1
.

For every matching cut
(A,B)
of a subcubic graph
G
, if
G[A]
contains at least one edge, then there exist a sequence of matching cuts

(A_{0},B_{0}),(A_{1},B_{1}),\dots,(A_{k},B_{k})

and a sequence of edges
a_{0}b_{0},a_{1}b_{1},\dots,a_{k-1}b_{k-1}
of
G
, with
(A_{0},B_{0})=(A,B)
, such that, for each
i<k
, the vertex
a_{i}
is red and
b_{i}
is blue with respect to
(A_{i},B_{i})
, and

A_{i+1}=A_{i}\oplus\left\{{a_{i},b_{i}}\right\}\quad\text{and}\quad B_{i+1}=B_{i}\oplus\left\{{a_{i},b_{i}}\right\}.

Moreover, with all colors interpreted with respect to either
(A_{k},B_{k})
or
(B_{k},A_{k})
, one of the graphs in
Figures

3
,
4
and
5
, or in
Figures

6
and
7
(respectively,
Figures

8
and
10
), is an induced subgraph of
G
satisfying the indicated degree constraints when present. ∎

The Lean source files are included in the ancillary directory
anc/lean
with the arXiv submission.
